### [AI will make biological extinction risks worse before it makes them better](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xdsvtuZBFipZYGjvb/ai-will-make-biological-extinction-risks-worse-before-it)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>An argument goes: If we don't build aligned artificial superintelligence, we risk driving ourselves extinct for some other reason. We should rush to build ASI quickly, in spite of the risks—the longer we wait, the more vulnerable we are to extinction from a different cause.</p>
<p>Other than ASI, the biggest extinction risk is synthetic biology. Some lab could (accidentally or on purpose) develop a highly transmissible, 100% fatal super-plague that wipes out humanity.</p>
<p>An aligned ASI could stop that from happening by shutting down dangerous biological research, or by developing advanced countermeasures that stop the spread of deadly infections. So the argument goes: We need to build ASI to save us from non-AI extinction risks.</p>
<p>However, that argument doesn't work. In the near term, AI will make biological risks <em>worse</em>, not better. AI will accelerate scientific research, which will bring us closer to the level of knowledge necessary to build extinction-level pathogens. And in the long term, the way ASI eliminates biological x-risk is by taking control of the world.</p>
<p><em>Cross-posted from <a href="https://mdickens.me/2026/06/29/AI_will_make_biorisk_worse_before_making_it_better/">my website</a>.</em></p>
<h2>In the near term, AI makes biorisk worse</h2>
<p>Some people imagine that AI models would accelerate defensive research while refusing to assist with developing bioweapons. This plan has two minor issues and one fatal one.</p>
<p>The first minor issue: Current AI model refusals are not robust, and there are workarounds to get information out of them for people who want to. It's very hard for AI developers to patch <em>all</em> holes, but the jailbreakers only need to find <em>one</em>.</p>
<p>The second minor issue: Even if the leading AI developer makes their model safe and un-jailbreakable, at least one of their competitors will probably fail at that task.</p>
<p>The fatal issue: It's not just about what AI assistants can do for humans. It's that <em>AI accelerates the rate of scientific progress</em>. As state of knowledge improves for <em>humanity in general</em>, it becomes possible for <em>humanity</em> to develop existentially risky pathogens, even if AI does not assist directly. It seems impossible to advance biological science while surgically preserving ignorance on just those bits of knowledge that are required to engineer pathogens.</p>
<p>AI might refuse to participate in gain-of-function research, and that would be better than not refusing. But suppose I'm an evil scientist and I want to develop a 100% lethal airborne pathogen. Here in the year 2026, I can't do it. Even if I'm on the cutting edge of medicine and biology, I still won't be able to create the "extinction pathogen", because that would require a level of scientific understanding that humanity simply hasn't achieved. If AI advances science <em>in general</em>, it will push me closer to my evil goal of killing everyone with bioweapons.</p>
<p>There is the question of "offense-defense balance": is it easier to develop deadly pathogens, or easier to protect people against pathogens? That question matters in many contexts, but it's not relevant here. At our current level of scientific understanding, we have ~zero ability to develop extinction-level bioweapons. If our understanding becomes sufficiently advanced, then that ability will move from zero to nonzero, regardless of the offense-defense balance.</p>
<p>Leaving AI out of the picture, humanity will probably have the knowledge necessary to make extinction-level pathogens within the next hundred years. If AI causes a hundred years of progress in the next decade, then the evil scientist will be able to engineer their extinction pathogen by 2036, thanks to AI—even if the AI itself doesn't directly participate in the creation of the pathogen.</p>
<p>By 2036, assuming AI hasn't killed us yet, biorisk will be higher than in the alternative 2036 where AI capabilities stopped improving. Would 2036-biorisk-with-AI be higher than 2126-biorisk-without-AI? Maybe not—maybe AI scientists would be safer than human scientists per unit of research effort. But at minimum, AI-accelerated science is more dangerous <em>per unit of time</em>. AI acceleration means the high-risk period starts sooner, and it means we have less time. Less time to identify risks, less time for policy-makers to respond, less time to consider what direction we should go in. Speedrunning through a century of progress in a decade makes it much harder to manage the risks as they come.</p>
<h2>AI can't control scientific progress unless it controls everything</h2>
<p>The only way to accelerate scientific progress in biology without increasing x-risk is for AI to have complete control over scientific capabilities—basically, it has to be impossible for any humans to use their increasingly-advanced knowledge of biology to develop bioweapons. I don't see how to do that unless all science is being done by AI, with humans not participating anymore.</p>
<p>Many people have a vision of the future in which humans will coexist with advanced AI, and we will remain in control of the steering wheel. But if humanity is in control, how can AI prevent us from developing powerful bioweapons? We can't have it both ways.</p>
<p>One might say, "Governments will have to prevent terrorist and mad scientists from developing bioweapons." To which I say, indeed they should do that. But AI makes governments' jobs harder on that front, not easier, unless AI has totalitarian grip on society—at which point we're back to the scenario where humans lose control over the future.</p>
<p>Another attempt at escaping the dilemma: Let the government control AI, and AI control everyone else. Even in the world where the government is democratically elected, that world is starting to sound like an extreme version of <a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/bad-definitions-of-democracy-and">Bad Definitions Of "Democracy" Shade Into Totalitarianism</a>, in which your life is fully controlled by AI, and the only time when you get any say in the matter is at the voting booth.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-TEtaFoixEdG2n5tww-1">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-TEtaFoixEdG2n5tww-1">[1]</a></sup>
    </span> I can imagine much worse outcomes than that, but it's not what I would describe as a happy ending.</p>
<h2>Low biorisk trades off against high AI takeover risk</h2>
<p>AI increases biorisk until it's powerful enough to completely shut down any danger. Therefore, the way to minimize AI-driven biological x-risk is to have a very short window of time between "AI is smart enough to accelerate biological research" and "superintelligent AI controls everything". But if that window is short, then we have little time to solve the alignment problem, and little time to steer AI while we are still in control of the future. AI-enhanced biorisk is lowest in the worlds where AI takeover risk is highest.</p>
<p>People with relatively low credence in AI takeover risk tend to expect a slow takeoff. But in a slow takeoff, AI makes biorisk worse well before it's smart enough to robustly prevent extinction-level pandemics.</p>
<h2>Accelerating AI development is not a good way to reduce biorisk</h2>
<p>We don't currently know how to build bioweapons that kill everyone, and eventually we will know how to do that.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-TEtaFoixEdG2n5tww-2">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-TEtaFoixEdG2n5tww-2">[2]</a></sup>
    </span> Much like how, in 1900, there was no risk of nuclear winter because we didn't yet know how to build nuclear weapons.</p>
<p>Scientific progress brings prosperity, but it can also enable dangerous new technologies. General biology research might even be harmful on balance due to increasing extinction risk—I don't have a well-informed view on whether that's true. What I <em>can</em> say is that the following argument does not hold up:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>We need to accelerate AI progress so that it can save us from biological extinction risks.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>Consider the neighboring argument, "we need to accelerate AI progress to create medical advancements." That argument is failing to do basic cost-benefit analysis (the risk of extinction is not outweighed by short-term improvements in medicine), but at least it's true that AI could, indeed, improve the state of medicine. "We should accelerate AI to reduce biological x-risk" isn't even clearly correct about the upside.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-TEtaFoixEdG2n5tww-3">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-TEtaFoixEdG2n5tww-3">[3]</a></sup>
    </span></p>
<h2>This is yet another illustration of the fact that we don't know what "aligned AI" means</h2>
<p>In the (possibly brief) window where AI is smart enough to do scientific research but doesn't yet control the whole world, AI increases biological x-risk by improving humanity's knowledge of how to develop powerful bioweapons. After that window, what happens? If we're in a world where ASI is powerful enough to reduce extinction risk to zero, what does that world look like, and what <em>should</em> it look like? I find it difficult to imagine what sort of radical transformations to civilization would be necessary to achieve a total elimination of x-risk.</p>
<p>Some people imagine a future where <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pQwNgB7ytwqTxxYue/dos-capital">everyone owns their own galaxy</a>. How can we make meaningful claims about x-risk when the future looks that <a href="https://mdickens.me/2026/03/29/future_will_be_weirder_than_that/">weird</a>? If I can own a galaxy (whatever that means), maybe some other person can deconstruct a handful of planets to build an army of 100% deadly super-nanoviruses and send them throughout the universe at 99.9999% the speed of light so that they kill everyone before anyone even sees them coming. Or something.</p>
<p>Many people have an intuition that aligned ASI will fix everything and the world will be great. But if we succeed at figuring out how to get ASI to do what we want, how do we then specify its behavior such that we get a good outcome? Some people hand-wave the problem away by saying "the ASI will be smart, it will help us figure out what to tell it to do." Much like <a href="https://mdickens.me/2025/11/27/alignment_bootstrapping_is_dangerous/">alignment bootstrapping</a>, this answer has a chicken-and-egg problem: how can the ASI figure out what you should tell it to do if you haven't yet told it how to determine what you should tell it to do?</p>
<p>(If an "assistant ASI" comes to you with some answer, and it's far smarter than you, how can you judge whether its answer is correct?)</p>
<p>The biorisk case is an example of the general problem that we don't know how to specify how an ASI should behave. Others have discussed this problem in more general terms, including:</p>
<ul>
<li><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/N6tsGwxaAo7iGTiBG/a-conflict-between-ai-alignment-and-philosophical-competence">A Conflict Between AI Alignment and Philosophical Competence</a> by Wei Dai (2025)</li>
<li><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rLd7NWNKnRFdnJEgD/intent-alignment-seems-incoherent">Intent alignment seems incoherent</a> by Joe Rogero (2025)</li>
<li><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FvERMXkaobQvdjS4q/many-individual-cevs-are-probably-quite-bad">Many individual CEVs are probably quite bad</a> by Villiam (2025)</li>
</ul>
<p>The concerns with biological x-risk are a specific illustration of the general problem. How, exactly, do you build an AI that prevents humans from killing each other with bioweapons, but without making things horrible as a side effect?</p>
<ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-TEtaFoixEdG2n5tww-1"><p>To be clear, I do not believe this scenario is at all likely. I'm using it as a hypothetical way of escaping the dilemma, to illustrate that even this "solution" still isn't something we want. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-TEtaFoixEdG2n5tww-1">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-TEtaFoixEdG2n5tww-2"><p>Unless AI kills us first. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-TEtaFoixEdG2n5tww-2">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-TEtaFoixEdG2n5tww-3"><p>This brings to mind an important (but off-topic) question: if scientific advancement increases existential risk, but it's also essential to improve standards of living, how should we proceed? We don't have an answer for that question yet, but whatever we come up with, I imagine it would be fair to summarize as: "We proceed carefully." As we learn more about what sorts of advancements are dangerous, we can implement mitigations.</p>
<p>If AI rapidly accelerates progress—even assuming AI itself doesn't kill everyone—then it will be difficult to implement mitigations as we go, because the time gap between "top scientists foresee a dangerous technology on the horizon" and "anyone can develop this technology in their garage" will become much shorter.</p>
<p>(Another possibility is that humanity <em>doesn't</em> solve the problem of how to advance science without introducing new x-risks. Instead, we solve AI alignment, and then AI solves every other problem.) <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-TEtaFoixEdG2n5tww-3">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
</ol>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xdsvtuZBFipZYGjvb/ai-will-make-biological-extinction-risks-worse-before-it#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [WSJ Article Claiming China Has Matched Anthropic Is Obvious Nonsense](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bpBYm5jiS4tpyzuDS/wsj-article-claiming-china-has-matched-anthropic-is-obvious)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/chinese-ai-anthropic-mythos-cybersecurity-574b02c2?mod=hp_trendingnow_article_pos2">The Wall Street Journal printed an outright false headline</a> and heavily misleading story claiming this, which of course was uncritically amplified by the usual suspects.</p>
<p>I post this now on its own so that we have a place to link to, to explain the situation.</p>


<h4>Headline News</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/chinese-ai-anthropic-mythos-cybersecurity-574b02c2?mod=hp_trendingnow_article_pos2">WSJ Headline</a> (Obvious Nonsense): ​China Has Matched Anthropic in Cybersecurity, Resetting AI Race.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/bpBYm5jiS4tpyzuDS/ifcjs9865xlxcjslabmp" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>&nbsp;</p></blockquote>
<p>That. Did. Not. Happen.</p>
<p>The post even claims, explicitly, that Claude Opus 4.8 similarly ‘matches’ Claude Mythos, a claim which is even more obviously false.</p>
<p>Shame upon the Wall Street Journal. I fear Gell-Mann Amnesia. If they can get something as important as this so completely wrong, what about everything else?</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25374"></span>


</div>
<p>I am skipping over the parts that involve accurate reporting, or minor quibbles.</p>
<p>It seems important to focus on clearly debunking the central false claims.</p>
<p>Alas, the mistakes made here very much rhyme with mistakes being made throughout all this by the White House, and that get latched onto by certain bad actors, who have played a large part in leaving us unprepared for the Mythos Moment.</p>
<p>For a full understanding of GLM-5.2, which is indeed an impressive open model, <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/glm-52-is-the-new-best-open-model"><strong>here is my full coverage of that release</strong></a>, placing it in proper context.</p>
<p>It is important to understand what makes Mythos special. This is not it.</p>


<h4>What Makes Mythos Special</h4>


<p>What makes mythos special is not that only the chosen one can identify any given vulnerability in code.</p>
<p><strong>What makes Mythos special is that it can identify vulnerabilities autonomously, at scale, without being pointed at them, and can then autonomously string together a variety of seemingly unrelated vulnerabilities into full working exploits.</strong></p>
<p>This is the thing that GPT-5.6 Sol cannot do, and that Opus 4.8 and GPT-5.5 cannot do, and that GLM-5.2 also cannot do, at anything like the same level of difficulty. It is also a thing that Fable cannot do, not with any known prompting strategy, whether or not you call this a ‘jailbreak.’</p>
<p>Mythos cannot do anything that you could not eventually do with a less capable model, in the same sense that an infinite number of monkeys can write Shakespeare.</p>
<p>The public would net benefit if both Fable and Sol were generally available in their current forms as soon as possible, including on the question of cybersecurity. Whereas releasing Mythos to the public at this time would in expectation be a serious error.</p>


<h4>Going Over The Detailed Claims</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/chinese-ai-anthropic-mythos-cybersecurity-574b02c2">Robert McMillan, Raffaele Huang and Amrith Ramkumar</a> (WSJ, being super misleading): Chinese artificial-intelligence systems have matched the performance of Anthropic’s powerful model Mythos in some cybersecurity scenarios, a development poised to reset the global tech race and pressure the White House in its <a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/trump-ai-anthropic-mythos-regulation-2378971f?&amp;mod=article_inline">overhaul of U.S. AI policy</a>.</p></blockquote>
<p>My lord, I hate the rules of <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/how-to-bounded-distrust">bounded distrust</a>, because the headline is allowed to lie (as it does here) and this first paragraph does not actually break journalistic norms.</p>
<p>Why? Technically speaking, yes, ‘in some cybersecurity scenarios’ Z.ai’s GLM-5.2 can match the performance of Mythos. Those scenarios could be called ‘the easy ones.’</p>
<blockquote><p>Security researchers said that a new AI model, released this month by China’s Zhipu AI, also known as Z.ai, can match the latest U.S. models when it comes to finding security bugs, although it still lags behind Anthropic’s and <a href="https://www.wsj.com/topics/subject/openai">OpenAI</a>’s products in other tasks.​</p></blockquote>
<p>More precisely, this is the same finding that was harped on previously about how various models, not only GPT-5.5 and Opus 4.8 but also some open ones, could always identify any given security issue in code, provided you give them sufficient resources and point them at the particular correct subsection of code.</p>
<p>I would challenge the idea that GLM-5.2 can match Mythos in security bug identification in practice, but again if we sufficiently narrowly define it, then most of the things that Mythos can find GLM-5.2 can, if pointed correctly, also find.</p>
<p>Onward to more skirting of the line. It’s almost an art form here.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/chinese-ai-anthropic-mythos-cybersecurity-574b02c2">Robert McMillan, Raffaele Huang and Amrith Ramkumar</a> (WSJ): Overall, the capability gap between top U.S. models and those built by Chinese companies has narrowed significantly, and use of Chinese AI systems has surged as businesses seek to rein in runaway costs. A host of companies, including <a href="https://www.wsj.com/market-data/quotes/MSFT">Microsoft</a>, are weighing how they can offer <a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/microsofts-satya-nadella-we-cant-let-ai-giants-eat-the-economy-b9d33b9f?mod=article_inline">Chinese models</a> on their platforms, a development that is set to alter the balance of power among tech companies.</p></blockquote>
<p>Use of AI systems of all types has surged, so Chinese model usage has as well. And yes, Microsoft considered using a Chinese model for Copilot, because Copilot is the enshittified product they offer and they want to save money. And ‘set to alter the balance of power’ does not have a magnitude attached, so it technically is true.</p>
<p>Then there’s the claim that the ‘capability gap has narrowed significantly,’ which is true in the sense that GLM-5.2 is considerably better than previous Chinese models, which means the gap shrunk a bunch the day of its release. The observed gap is going to continuously move both up and down, and there will be some troughs along the way in terms of nowcast gaps.</p>
<p>The impression here and later, that the gap is predictably shrinking over time, is simply false. Before GLM-5.2, I would say the gap had clearly gotten larger, both in terms of number of cycles and also absolute clock time, since DeepSeek’s R1 and the DeepSeek moment, which itself was not especially close.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/chinese-ai-anthropic-mythos-cybersecurity-574b02c2">Robert McMillan, Raffaele Huang and Amrith Ramkumar</a> (WSJ): “China is making sure that the gap becomes smaller and smaller over time,” said Lior Div, chief executive officer of the cybersecurity company 7AI.</p></blockquote>
<p>You’re always allowed to quote someone else, but Lior Div is incorrect here.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/chinese-ai-anthropic-mythos-cybersecurity-574b02c2">Robert McMillan, Raffaele Huang and Amrith Ramkumar</a> (WSJ): On Wednesday, the Chinese cybersecurity company <a href="https://www.wsj.com/market-data/quotes/CN/XSHG/601360">360 Security Technology</a> released a new bug-finding tool called Tulongfeng. The company said it was comparable to Mythos in finding bugs. Those capabilities have alarmed many national-security officials and CEOs.</p></blockquote>
<p>Again, the company presumably did say this. But why would you believe them? This kind of false or heavily misleading claim is made all the time.</p>


<h4>One Helpful Note</h4>


<p>I do want to note one helpful thing that was said:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/chinese-ai-anthropic-mythos-cybersecurity-574b02c2">Robert McMillan, Raffaele Huang and Amrith Ramkumar</a> (WSJ): “Banning Fable while selling chips China needs to develop its own version is a gift to China,” said Saif Khan, a distinguished technology fellow at the Institute for Progress think tank who worked on export restrictions in the Biden administration. The U.S. needs to maximize the use of Mythos and comparable models to harden its cyber defenses while it can, he added.</p></blockquote>
<p>Okay, yeah, that paragraph is accurate and important, so at least there’s that.</p>


<h4>The Overall Impression Is Extremely Wrong</h4>


<p>But the overall impression of the article, even excluding the headline, is that China is steadily catching up if it has not already done so, and even that it has matched Mythos where it counts and in the capabilities that caused the White House to delay and restrict its release, and that Chinese open models are steadily taking over AI usage. All of this is completely wrong.</p>
<p>There is a reason that <a href="https://x.com/Tim_Hua_/status/2071098916272275470">Tim Hua was among those having very unkind words here</a>.</p>


<h4>All Of This Has Happened Before And Will Happen Again</h4>


<p>This was not the first rather importantly false headline in the WSJ about AI.</p>
<p>See ‘<a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/anthropic-urges-global-pause-in-ai-development-flags-self-improvement-risk-99cefb73?mod=series_chatgptai">Anthropic Urges Global Pause in AI Development, Flags ‘Self-Improvement Risk</a>,’ from June 4, 2026. Anthropic came very short of any such suggestion. In that past case, the body of the article was fine, it was only the headline that was false, but that is one hell of a false impression to put onto readers.</p>
<p>We also have had to suffer quite a lot of ‘Chinese models have caught up’ headlines, from various sources. Some day one of them might be true. Today is not that day.</p>
<p>Thank you for your attention to this matter.</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bpBYm5jiS4tpyzuDS/wsj-article-claiming-china-has-matched-anthropic-is-obvious#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [P(doom) is a Dumb Meme](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/6h7aAd4aw8YgCAbF6/p-doom-is-a-dumb-meme)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Look, I'm as much of a Rationalist with a special interest in AI x-risk as anyone. But </span><i><span>oh my god </span></i><span>do I hate talking about "P(doom)". When it gained widespread usage in the wake of ChatGPT, I assumed that it was floating around variously adjacent circles of faux-intellectuals, but surely everyone in my circles could see how braindead it was... right?</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1782339549/lexical_client_uploads/wbeaggifsnw9iekovwtl.png" /><figcaption><p><span>(This post was partially inspired by a recent conversation with Liron about </span><a href="https://lironshapira.substack.com/" rel="noreferrer"><span>Doom Debates</span></a><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref9xu8oaavsng"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn9xu8oaavsng">[1]</a></sup></span><span>)</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>I guess it's time for me to </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Zp6wG5eQFLGWwcG6j/focus-on-the-places-where-you-feel-shocked-everyone-s" rel="noreferrer"><span>focus on a place where I'm shocked that everyone else is dropping the ball</span></a><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefytsq291e1t"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnytsq291e1t">[2]</a></sup></span></p><h2><span>P(doom) is Hopelessly Vague</span></h2><p><span>Let's start with the ambiguity. Does "doom" mean... extinction? A lot of people think so! I have personally encountered people who think </span><i><span>catastrophic </span></i><span>harms from AI are likely, but the risks of </span><i><span>all </span></i><span>humans dying are low. They're like "Sure, 99.999% of humans might die from AI, but the AI will obviously want to keep thousands of humans alive for science and potential trade with aliens and stuff, so my P(doom) is approximately 0%."</span></p><p><span>That might sound crazy. Surely you, dear reader, know exactly what "doom" means. You know, for example, which of these count as doom and which don't:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>A young ASI tries to use it's first-mover advantage to take over the world and prevent other ASI competitors from emerging. In doing so it sparks a war against humanity where it eventually loses,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefz4zif4c87io"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnz4zif4c87io">[3]</a></sup></span><span> but it kills 10% of all humans in the process.</span></li><li value="2"><span>ASI empowers a single person or small group of humans to become tyrants and lock in a permanent authoritarian regime where almost all other humans are subject to the whims of the tyrant(s).</span></li><li value="3"><span>A state actor or terrorist group uses </span><i><span>narrow </span></i><span>AI to build a bioweapon (eg AlphaFold but for plagues), and that weapon gets out and kills literally everyone.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefn90zdpb6sxa"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnn90zdpb6sxa">[4]</a></sup></span></li><li value="4"><span>A great power conflict starts for reasons mostly unrelated to AI, but advanced AI systems are deployed on the various battlefields, and eventually (in part due to the speed and ruthlessness of AI) it escalates to a thermonuclear war that kills 99% of humanity.</span></li><li value="5"><span>Humans "</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pZerSnxv6FPqvgoYu/book-review-age-of-em" rel="noreferrer"><span>merge with the machines</span></a><span>" in a way that, from the outside, looks an awful lot like all of humanity being subsumed by inhuman machinery.</span></li><li value="6"><span>Humans become incapable of competing with machines in almost all sectors of the economy. While the rule of law persists, the political landscape is also dominated by a variety of ASIs with various goals, and they don't redistribute significant wealth into human hands. Only a small number of humans survive in the long-run, relegated to being a historical curiosity.</span></li><li value="7"><span>ASIs are developed in a broadly corrigible way, leading to extreme abundance, and the offense-defense balance means that the world is basically safe. But everyone (even the Amish) eventually stop having human kids because other things (including AI "kids") are way more fun/satisfying. Even though lifespans are really long, people still gradually die off, and eventually only the machines remain.</span></li></ul><p><span>Implicit in all of this are questions of timeframe. Many people that I've talked to feel that </span><a href="https://gradual-disempowerment.ai/" rel="noreferrer"><span>gradual disempowerment</span></a><span> probably doesn't count as AI doom because it's too slow, and they're assuming a short timeframe for "P(doom)" like 5 or 10 years. Others assume that P(doom) means "this century." (But does that mean the next 74 years or 100 years?) Does the heat death of the universe count as doom?</span></p><p><span>Even if you have a way that you like to think about the subject, are you sure that the person you're talking with is using the same frame? If I've learned one thing from making lots of bets and engaging with prediction markets nearly every day for years, it's that the devil is in the details, and that finding the right way to operationalize a bet is often the hardest part.</span></p><p><span>(Edit: To be clear, I am not saying that vague statements are always bad. There's only so much communication bandwidth, after all. What I am against is vagueness under the guise of precision, where people don't realize that they have different interpretations, and thus miscommunicate.)</span></p><h2><span>Inside Views, Outside Views, and Likelihood Ratios</span></h2><blockquote><p><i><span>"I actually think the risk [from AGI] is more than 50% ... but I don't say that because there's other people think it's less, and I think a sort of plausible thing that takes into account the opinions of everybody I know is sort of 10 to 20%."</span></i><br /><span>                                                                                          — </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PTF5Up1hMhw&amp;t=2283s" rel="noreferrer"><span>Geoffrey Hinton, 2024</span></a></p></blockquote><p><span>Let's say that you're trying to estimate the number of beans in a jar. A good way to do that is use the wisdom of crowds. Go around to a bunch of people, ask them to guess, and then take the average of their guesses. Some people will be too high, others will be too low, but in theory their guesses will be correlated with the truth and their errors will be independent, so when you average them, the errors cancel out.</span></p><p><span>But now suppose that you go up to someone and ask them to write their guess on a piece of paper. They write down 1000. Then, you go to the Nobel Prize winning "godfather" of bean-counting. He's about to write 5000, but then spots the "1000" at the top of the page. "I'm probably too high," he says to himself, and writes 3000 instead, thinking himself very clever. And indeed, he is clever! If all that mattered was the accuracy of </span><i><span>his </span></i><span>guess, then updating on the evidence is smart.</span></p><p><span>But if you then repeat this process and get a bunch of guesses, then average them, you'll do way worse. You're double-counting evidence! The errors in the first guess get compounded in the second guess, and at that point who wants to go against the expert consensus? People look around them and assume that a </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/2017/10/13/fire-alarm/" rel="noreferrer"><span>fire alarm</span></a><span> can't possibly be going off, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Hoh6umyMWSqzPGMJZ/social-behavior-curves-equilibria-and-radicalism" rel="noreferrer"><span>because not enough people are acting</span></a><span>, not realizing that most other people aren't acting for a similar reason.</span></p><p><span>These two forms of probability — </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/gears-level" rel="noreferrer"><span>gearsy</span></a><span> inside-views and all-things-considered </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/BcYfsi7vmhDvzQGiF/taboo-outside-view" rel="noreferrer"><span>outside-views</span></a><span> — can disagree wildly! When I try to tell a concrete story of the development of superintelligence going well, not fudging or assuming some mysterious breakthrough, I simply fail. In that sense, my P(doom) is 100%. That's the number that I claim you should take from me if you're calculating an average. But I am also a Bayesian, and thus entirely aware that 100% isn't a valid probability. When adopting a stance of humility that keeps my ignorance in mind, my sense of being doomed goes down a lot.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmzgaczvwkar"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmzgaczvwkar">[5]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>But </span><i><span>both</span></i><span> of these numbers are usually a distraction!</span></p><p><span>When I'm talking to someone about the risk from AI, I don't really want to average my worldview with theirs. What I want to do is learn their insights. Insight can come in the form of evidence, experienced out in the world, or it can come from reasoning and studying the ideas in question. Insight gives </span><a href="https://www.overcomingbias.com/p/share-likelihood-ratios-not-posterior-beliefshtml" rel="noreferrer"><span>an update in the form of a likelihood ratio</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>Imagine a person named Gloomer who has a naturally sad disposition, perhaps because of his genes, or maybe they had a rotten childhood. Gloomer starts with a prior of 99:1 that humanity will go extinct in his lifetime (inside view). Again, this isn't really based on anything. He's just a pessimist. Now suppose that Gloomer learns of an alignment technique that has promise, according to his understanding of the science, but doesn't solve the whole problem. Being the pessimist that he is, he suspects that the rest of the problem probably won't get solved in time. And even if it did, something else would probably get us.</span></p><p><span>If you ask Gloomer for his P(doom), even if you're very clear to operationalize it in the right way, defining your terms and asking for their inside view, he might say "9:1." Yikes! That's pretty bleak! But now imagine asking "in what ways have you updated about doom?" Gloomer might tell you about becoming less pessimistic, sharing his 11:1 evidence that things are going to be okay!</span></p><p><span>In my general experience, conversations about reason and evidence ("Why do you believe what you believe?") rather than bottom-line conclusions ("What do you believe?") get into productive territory much faster, and devolve less often into performative bafflement around the other person's bottom-line.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1782494917/lexical_client_uploads/iwcv7le2dld7v1sqx6ht.png" /><h2><span>P(doom) is Fatalistic</span></h2><p><span>What is the probability that you will say "Zimbabwe," out-loud to yourself, in the next 60 seconds?</span></p><p><span>Probabilistic reasoning is a (mental) tool. And like all tools, it has places where it fits nicely and helps you do work, and has places where, if you insist on applying it, you'll make a mess. In particular, it carries a flavor of looking at things from the outside, like a detached spectator betting on the way a sports match will go. But as the saying goes: There are two ways to be right. You can be right because you're a wizard, and have thus found the secret truths of reality, or you can be right because you're a king, and have decided that things will be the way you say they are.</span></p><p><span>Some secret observer might do well to speculate about whether you'll say "Zimbabwe" in terms of probability, but </span><i><span>you</span></i><span> should instead ask yourself "What do I want to say?"</span></p><p><span>This is especially important in the context of coordination problems and multi-party interactions. For example, if I said to my wife "I think there's an 8% chance we'll get divorced in the next five years," the very act of sharing that prediction might cause that number to go up! She might reasonably interpret it as a sign that I don't </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/duvzdffTzL3dWJcxn/believing-in-1" rel="noreferrer"><span>believe in</span></a><span> the partnership. Then, she might respond with her own prediction of 14%, leading me to update in the same way, in a back-and-forth pattern that ends up in divorce. This cascade of updates is only irrational in that </span><b><span>speaking purely in terms of high-level probabilities is the wrong tool for the job of coordination</span></b><span>. We could dodge it by getting specific, asking to talk about likelihood ratios and what sorts of things might doom the marriage. Or we could dodge it by asking "Do you also want to be married?", taking the other person's "Yes" as good enough, and moving on with our lives, having jointly made that decision.</span></p><p><span>When leaders of companies or nations talk about how they don't expect other companies or nations to slow down in the race towards the AGI cliff, consider that this expression of pessimism might be a self-fulfilling prophesy. Those words are a signal to those who are listening that the speaker doesn't expect (and therefore doesn't intend!) to cooperate.</span></p><p><span>Perversely, the inverse dynamic can also happen! I've met people who say they have a </span><i><span>low</span></i><span> P(doom) because "Obviously it would be extremely dangerous to charge ahead, even as superintelligence gets closer. Humanity isn't that stupid. We'll slow down and figure things out, and thereby avoid doom."</span></p><p><span>But, uhh... do you realize that giving a low P(doom) is communicating the exact opposite of your worldview?! The situation resembles Murphy's Law: if you believe that things will go fine, your carelessness may cause things to fail; if you believe that things will go wrong, you will prepare, and by preparing, things will go fine.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefihpvcvr2usp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnihpvcvr2usp">[6]</a></sup></span><span> The situation isn't paradoxical. You just have to have believe that </span><i><span>conditional on being cautious</span></i><span>, things will turn out okay. Giving a non-conditional probability hides our power, and ability to act with caution, implying that things will either be fine (in which case why bother taking action) or they won't (in which case why bother taking action) or they are up to chance (in which case why bother taking action).</span></p><p><span>Some people feel powerless. I get that. They don't feel like they get to decide whether humanity is cautious or reckless. They don't feel like, by vocalizing hope or fear, they are changing the world. And sometimes that's right. It can be useful to quietly ask yourself whether cooperation is likely, or whether humanity will decide to slow down. Not everyone is in a position of power.</span></p><p><span>But </span><a href="https://utopiandreams.substack.com/p/decision-theory" rel="noreferrer"><span>our decisions are usually entangled</span></a><span> in more ways than we appreciate. You are part of a culture, a community, and a country. It matters, in aggregate, what people in those groups say and believe and try to accomplish. This aggregate will isn't some mysterious thing, beyond your power. It comes from you, and people like you. Every time you act, you are acting collectively.</span></p><p><span>At the level of humanity, at the very least, we are in control of Earth. If we decide to work together, we can work together. If we decide not to build machines that make us obsolete, we can (for now) just stop, and choose not to go down that road. Perhaps there are too many fools for things to work out, but assuming that to be the case (or saying things in public that imply that you think it) is itself a kind of foolishness.</span></p><h2><span>Counterarguments</span></h2><p><span>There are people I respect who disagree with me about P(doom) being a bad meme. Zvi Mowshowitz, for example, argued with me at LessOnline that while I'm not wrong about all the ways that it's vague and unsophisticated, it has the virtue of being memetically catchy enough to get people thinking about existential risk </span><i><span>at all</span></i><span>, whereas the default is that it gets ignored. He followed up by asking whether political prediction markets (ie "P(Trump)") are also bad.</span></p><p><span>These are strong counterpoints, and I take them seriously, but I'm ultimately convinced by neither.</span></p><p><span>On the question of memetic fitness, I agree that some people who might otherwise be oblivious, or swept up in some other memetic fad, probably spend more time thinking about the possibility of AI causing a disaster as a result of the meme. This is good. We need more people thinking about and talking about risks.</span></p><p><span>But it's a false dichotomy to choose between P(doom) and nothing. Even before P(doom), there was the obnoxious "what are your timelines?" meme</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefttbx4bk3mj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnttbx4bk3mj">[7]</a></sup></span><span> and many others before it. After, we had </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/2025/04/09/thoughts-on-ai-2027/" rel="noreferrer"><span>AI 2027</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2RwDgMXo6nh42egoC/how-to-game-the-metr-plot" rel="noreferrer"><span>the METR plot</span></a><span>, and of course:</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1782503560/lexical_client_uploads/mbmqj3vzh1odi0zwwene.png" /><p><span>Each of these have pros and cons, being variously sophisticated, memetically fit, and useful for having productive conversations. I'm not really saying that any of them are necessarily better than P(doom) — even though they are — but rather that a dumb meme is still a dumb meme even if it's better than nothing. We can, and should, do better!</span></p><p><span>One example of doing better are prediction markets, which I am broadly in favor of. Talking about probabilities and making bets seems to me to be a vastly superior way to do political forecasting than the raw punditry I grew up with. So what about "P(Trump)" and other such things? Why don't I have the same animosity for prediction markets as I do for P(doom)?</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Election markets aren't vague. Most are extremely well operationalized, with clear criteria and timelines. If someone made a market that was similarly vague, and people kept talking about it ad nauseam, I would have similar complaints.</span></li><li value="2"><span>In my view, people who are savvy enough to be thinking about prediction markets (outside of sports betting) are usually savvy enough to understand that something like "P(Trump)" exists in the context of a marketplace, and that knowing how someone bet on that market gives only a very limited handle on their beliefs. If people started implying that people with a high P(Trump) should be labeled as "Trumpers," I would also yell at them.</span></li><li value="3"><span>When people cite prediction markets (or more typically, polling) as a reason not to vote for the best candidate,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefxjp5nr89ai"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnxjp5nr89ai">[8]</a></sup></span><span> I think this is a dumb and bad mistake that is directly analogous to the fatalism surrounding P(doom)! Thankfully, it seems to me that most people understand that their vote matters for deciding elections, even though the market isn't conditional on one course of action or another.</span></li></ul><p><span>In short, P(Trump) isn't a </span><i><span>meme</span></i><span> that's rolling around the discourse, causing miscommunication and sloppy thinking. If it was, I'd probably be against it!</span></p><h2><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Nu3wa6npK4Ry66vFp/a-sense-that-more-is-possible" rel="noreferrer"><span>A Sense That More Is (Memetically) Possible</span></a></h2><p><span>P(doom) probably isn't going away, this side of the singularity. It's reached critical mass, and will get parroted by midwits, if nothing else. But </span><i><b><span>you </span></b></i><b><span>don't have to be a midwit!</span></b><span> You can recognize that P(doom) has the flavor of a </span><a href="https://slatestarcodex.com/2018/10/30/sort-by-controversial/" rel="noreferrer"><span>scissor statement</span></a><span>, rather than a nuanced perspective on reality. If someone brings it up in conversation, use it as an opportunity to get into the details of what sorts of futures they're concretely imagining, what they're basing that perspective on, and what sorts of things can be done to steer our fate. Refuse to just give simple numbers until things have been sufficiently operationalized. Take the road of rationality, and choose your </span><a href="https://x.com/AISafetyMemes" rel="noreferrer"><span>memes</span></a><span> wisely.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1782347743/lexical_client_uploads/kjbnaxqxjvfkywerei2j.png" /><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn9xu8oaavsng"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref9xu8oaavsng">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I gave a mini-version of this rant when </span><a href="https://lironshapira.substack.com/p/the-ai-corrigibility-debate-miri" rel="noreferrer"><span>I went on his show</span></a><span> late last year. While the P(doom) emphasis rubs me the wrong way, I like Doom Debates overall.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnytsq291e1t"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefytsq291e1t">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>To be fair, some others,</span><a href="https://x.com/ESYudkowsky/status/1823530314087718982" rel="noreferrer"><span> including Eliezer</span></a><span>, have also complained about it publicly.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnz4zif4c87io"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefz4zif4c87io">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>In the hypothetical, we can imagine the ASI is the weakest/stupidest possible AI that has a real shot at takeover. But thanks to it being right on the cusp, its success isn't guaranteed, and we happen to get lucky.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnn90zdpb6sxa"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefn90zdpb6sxa">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Perhaps by causing society to collapse to the point where the few stragglers in bunkers can't recover.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnmzgaczvwkar"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefmzgaczvwkar">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>How much? It depends on what "doom" means, of course! 😛</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnihpvcvr2usp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefihpvcvr2usp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This idea, and the difference between wizard correctness and kingly correctness, are ideas I picked up from CFAR, with particular thanks to Duncan Sabien. I'm not sure who the first person to develop the ideas was.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnttbx4bk3mj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefttbx4bk3mj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>"What are your timelines?" is bad for similar reasons. It's vague about what we're talking about. It encourages focusing on bottom-lines, rather than reason or evidence. It denies agency and our power to choose. And it, in practice, often resulted in point estimates, rather than distributions. Like with P(doom), there's </span><a href="https://www.aifutures.org/" rel="noreferrer"><span>a sophisticated way to do forecasting</span></a><span>, which the meme version turns into a cartoon.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnxjp5nr89ai"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefxjp5nr89ai">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>To be clear, I think that some amount of strategic voting is wise. In particular, as long as one is tracking their decision-theoretic reference class and the higher-order effects, I think it's good to try to estimate how the other voters are leaning and occasionally make compromises that look like voting for the lesser evil. I am rallying against the braindead "What can one vote do?" and "I live in a state where my vote doesn't matter!" narratives.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/6h7aAd4aw8YgCAbF6/p-doom-is-a-dumb-meme#comments">Discuss</a>

### [A reading list for generalists](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sH4cFDDjRdGrn3p2o/a-reading-list-for-generalists)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>I, along with many others in AI safety, believe there is a shortage of generalists in the community and that there exist many projects and efforts that by default will not happen unless they are owned by a strong generalist</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefgi0z1cfmy2"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fngi0z1cfmy2">[1]</a></sup></span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref1aq112mglff"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn1aq112mglff">[2]</a></sup></span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefp1rcbg72z6t"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnp1rcbg72z6t">[3]</a></sup></span><span>. As someone who is a reasonably good generalist, I decided to assemble a reading list of the essays and blog posts that have personally helped me the most. </span><b><span>I would love others to comment with pieces they think should be on this list.</span></b></p><p><span>The crux of this reading list is the idea that if you’re working hard as a generalist on a project you care a lot about, then by rigorously applying the lessons from these documents you will improve more quickly than you otherwise would. </span></p><p><span>By the numbers:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>I’ve attached 18 documents to start this reading list.</span></li><li value="2"><span>The authors cited more than once are Paul Graham (5), Ben Kuhn (4), Ethan Perez (2), and Greg Brockman (2). Sam Altman and Eliezer Yudkowsky also have their fingerprints over a lot of the content.</span></li><li value="3"><span>The items are 15 blog posts, 1 blog comment, 1 interview transcript in blog post form, and 1 book.</span></li></ul><hr /><h2><span>Dispositional</span></h2><p><span>What characteristics should you try to adopt?</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Paul Graham: "What We Look for in Founders" (</span><a href="https://paulgraham.com/founders.html"><span>link</span></a><span>), "Relentlessly Resourceful" (</span><a href="https://www.paulgraham.com/relres.html"><span>link</span></a><span>)</span></li><li value="2"><span>Eliezer Yudkowsky: "Shut Up and Do the Impossible!" (</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nCvvhFBaayaXyuBiD/shut-up-and-do-the-impossible"><span>link</span></a><span>)</span></li><li value="3"><span>Ben Kuhn: "Be impatient" (</span><a href="https://www.benkuhn.net/impatient/"><span>link</span></a><span>)</span></li><li value="4"><span>Cate Hall: "How to be more agentic" (</span><a href="https://usefulfictions.substack.com/p/how-to-be-more-agentic"><span>link</span></a><span>)</span></li></ul><h2><span>Strategy</span></h2><p><span>How do you make good decisions with the information you have, and how can you get the additional information you need?</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Anna Salamon: "Humans are not automatically strategic" (</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PBRWb2Em5SNeWYwwB/humans-are-not-automatically-strategic"><span>link</span></a><span>)</span><ul><li value="1"><span>If I were to recommend one single item from this list it would be this one because 1) it’s good to understand the ways in which otherwise-intelligent people are unstrategic, and 2) it’s good to understand the ways in which </span><i><span>you</span></i><span> are not automatically strategic. I have gotten a ton of mileage in my short career thus far simply by being more strategic. The defining trait of the most effective coworker I’ve ever had is that he is unrelentingly strategic.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>Holden Karnofsky: "Learning By Writing" (</span><a href="https://www.cold-takes.com/learning-by-writing/"><span>link</span></a><span>)</span></li><li value="3"><span>Ethan Perez with Mikita Balesni and Henry Sleight: "How I select alignment research projects" (</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/7GmDs4BqrFW3kk4nP/how-i-select-alignment-research-projects"><span>link</span></a><span>)</span></li><li value="4"><span>Paul Graham: "Do Things that Don’t Scale" (</span><a href="https://paulgraham.com/ds.html"><span>link</span></a><span>)</span><ul><li value="1"><span>In particular, do things that give you maximal information.</span></li></ul></li><li value="5"><span>Ben Kuhn: "Impact, agency, and taste" (</span><a href="https://www.benkuhn.net/impact/"><span>link</span></a><span>)</span></li><li value="6"><span>Richard Ngo: comment on "Against Almost Every Theory of Impact of Interpretability" (</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LNA8mubrByG7SFacm/against-almost-every-theory-of-impact-of-interpretability-1?commentId=PazvJmFcv4DBXepzi"><span>link</span></a><span>)</span><ul><li value="1"><span>The original post is great but domain-specific. Ngo’s response shows the more general principle that winning whack-a-mole doesn’t conclusively discredit forward-chainy approaches.</span></li></ul></li></ul><h2><span>Project leadership</span></h2><p><span>How do you get projects done when your boots are on the ground?</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Ben Kuhn: "How I've run major projects" (</span><a href="https://www.benkuhn.net/pjm/"><span>link</span></a><span>)</span></li><li value="2"><span>Ben Kuhn: "Categories of leadership on technical teams" (</span><a href="https://www.benkuhn.net/leadcats/"><span>link</span></a><span>)</span></li><li value="3"><span>Greg Brockman: "</span><span>#define</span><span> CTO" (</span><a href="https://blog.gregbrockman.com/figuring-out-the-cto-role-at-stripe"><span>link</span></a><span>), "</span><span>#define</span><span> CTO OpenAI" (</span><a href="https://blog.gregbrockman.com/define-cto-openai"><span>link</span></a><span>)</span><ul><li value="1"><span>I think these posts are especially useful in showing the process a person with a spiky skillset can take to find their place in an organization. </span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span>Ethan Perez: "Tips for Empirical Alignment Research" (</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/dZFpEdKyb9Bf4xYn7/tips-for-empirical-alignment-research"><span>link</span></a><span>)</span><ul><li value="1"><span>Some of the advice is domain-specific to AI safety research, but the document as a whole provides great guidance on becoming an excellent IC, in particular an excellent </span><i><span>managed</span></i><span> IC, across any domain. Some good bits are the ones about making your work legible to others and scoping your project timelines appropriately.</span></li></ul></li></ul><h2><span>Interpersonal/organizational</span></h2><p><span>How can you understand the structure of an organization and contribute positively?</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Kevin Simler &amp; Robin Hanson: </span><i><span>The Elephant in the Brain</span></i><span> (</span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Elephant_in_the_Brain"><span>link</span></a><span>)</span><ul><li value="1"><span>The Elephant in the Brain ended up being the best book for organizational psychology by virtue of being the best book for human psychology in general. I think that the first section which goes over the broader theory of signaling is the important part to read. Understanding the values and motives of the people around you can help greatly in navigating complicated settings, but of equal importance is understanding that the core lens also applies to you: you’re a silly monkey trying to signal its genetic fitness via unstated means in order to increase status and sexual marketplace value like the rest of us.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>Martin Sustrik: "Accountability Sinks" (</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nYJaDnGNQGiaCBSB5/accountability-sinks"><span>link</span></a><span>)</span></li><li value="3"><span>Paul Graham: "Keep Your Identity Small" (</span><a href="https://www.paulgraham.com/identity.html"><span>link</span></a><span>)</span></li><li value="4"><span>Paul Graham: "How to Disagree" (</span><a href="https://www.paulgraham.com/disagree.html"><span>link</span></a><span>)</span></li></ul><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fngi0z1cfmy2"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefgi0z1cfmy2">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/k3nq7FxBCsrNFmAYi/ai-safety-s-biggest-talent-gap-isn-t-researchers-it-s-2"><span>https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/k3nq7FxBCsrNFmAYi/ai-safety-s-biggest-talent-gap-isn-t-researchers-it-s-2</span></a></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn1aq112mglff"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref1aq112mglff">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://www.matsprogram.org/tracks/founding-and-field-building"><span>https://www.matsprogram.org/tracks/founding-and-field-building</span></a></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnp1rcbg72z6t"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefp1rcbg72z6t">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://nanransohoff.substack.com/p/there-should-be-general-managers"><span>https://nanransohoff.substack.com/p/there-should-be-general-managers</span></a></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sH4cFDDjRdGrn3p2o/a-reading-list-for-generalists#comments">Discuss</a>
